Image name: Tu_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 9
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are G and I. My task is to find the minimum number of non-black nodes to remove to disconnect G from I.

First, I will identify all possible paths from G to I.
The neighbors of G are H, K, and F.
The neighbors of I are H, P, J, and C.

Let's categorize paths based on the first node from G:

**1. Paths starting with G-H:**
   *   G - H - I (Direct path)
   *   G - H - C - I (Path through C)

**2. Paths starting with G-K:**
   *   From K, the only connection is to X.
   *   From X, the only connection is to F.
   *   So, any path starting G-K must go through G-K-X-F.
   *   Examples:
      *   G - K - X - F - P - I
      *   G - K - X - F - P - J - I
      *   G - K - X - F - E - P - I
      *   G - K - X - F - E - P - J - I
      *   G - K - X - F - E - B - C - I (and variations through N/O to C)

**3. Paths starting with G-F:**
   *   From F, connections are to G, K, X, P, E.
   *   Examples:
      *   G - F - P - I
      *   G - F - P - J - I
      *   G - F - E - P - I
      *   G - F - E - P - J - I
      *   G - F - E - B - C - I (and variations through N/O to C)

Now, I will systematically check for a minimum cut set.

**Can a single node disconnect G and I?**
*   If I remove H: Paths G-H-I and G-H-C-I are broken. However, paths like G-F-P-I or G-K-X-F-E-B-C-I still exist. So, H alone is not enough.
*   If I remove F: Paths G-F-... are broken. Also, all paths starting G-K-X-... must pass through F (G-K-X-F-...). So, all paths starting G-F or G-K are broken. However, paths like G-H-I or G-H-C-I still exist. So, F alone is not enough.
*   If I remove K: Paths G-K-... are broken. However, paths like G-H-I, G-F-P-I, etc., still exist. So, K alone is not enough.
*   If I remove P: Paths G-...-P-I and G-...-P-J-I are broken. However, paths like G-H-I or G-H-C-I or G-F-E-B-C-I still exist. So, P alone is not enough.
*   If I remove J: Paths G-...-P-J-I are broken. However, paths like G-H-I or G-F-P-I or G-F-E-B-C-I still exist. So, J alone is not enough.
*   If I remove C: Paths G-...-C-I are broken. However, paths like G-H-I or G-F-P-I or G-F-E-P-I still exist. So, C alone is not enough.
*   Similarly, removing X, E, B, N, O, Y, L, M, D will not disconnect G and I, as there will always be alternative paths.

Since no single node can disconnect G and I, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

**Can two nodes disconnect G and I?**
Let's consider the "bottleneck" nodes that all paths from G must pass through.
All paths from G must start with G-H, G-K, or G-F.
*   If a path starts G-H, it uses H.
*   If a path starts G-K, it must go G-K-X-F, so it uses F.
*   If a path starts G-F, it uses F.

This means that every single path from G to I must pass through either node H or node F.
Let's verify this:
1.  Any path starting G-H: uses H.
2.  Any path starting G-K: must go G-K-X-F... so it uses F.
3.  Any path starting G-F: uses F.

Therefore, if I remove both node H and node F, all paths from G to I will be broken.
*   Removing H breaks all paths starting G-H.
*   Removing F breaks all paths starting G-F.
*   Removing F also breaks all paths starting G-K-X-F... because F is a necessary intermediate node for these paths to reach the rest of the graph towards I.

Thus, removing nodes H and F will completely disconnect G from I. This is a cut set of size 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [F, H]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 9
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and I.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node G is connected to: K, X, F, E, H.
    *   Node I is connected to: H, E, J, P, C, O.

2.  **Analyze the graph structure to find potential "bottlenecks":**
    I observe that nodes E and H are directly connected to both G and I, and also serve as crucial intermediate nodes for other paths. Let's consider the subgraph containing G and its immediate neighbors that are not E or H. This subgraph is {G, K, X, F}.

3.  **Identify all edges connecting the G-side of the graph to the I-side:**
    Any path from G to I must eventually leave the cluster {G, K, X, F} and enter the rest of the graph that leads to I. The nodes E and H appear to be the primary gateways.
    Let's list all edges that connect a node in {G, K, X, F} to a node outside this set (which could lead to I):
    *   (G, E): Connects G to E.
    *   (G, H): Connects G to H.
    *   (F, E): Connects F (which is connected to G) to E.
    *   (K, G), (K, X), (K, F): These are internal to {G, K, X, F}.
    *   (X, G), (X, K), (X, F): These are internal to {G, K, X, F}.

    Therefore, the only edges that connect the {G, K, X, F} cluster to the rest of the graph (which contains I) are (G, E), (G, H), and (F, E).

4.  **Propose a cut set based on these "gateway" edges:**
    If we remove the edges (G, E), (G, H), and (F, E), then:
    *   G can no longer reach E directly.
    *   G can no longer reach H directly.
    *   F can no longer reach E.
    *   Since K and X are only connected to G and F within this cluster, they also cannot reach E or H.
    *   As I is connected to E and H (among others), and E and H are the only ways for the {G, K, X, F} cluster to connect to the rest of the graph leading to I, removing these three edges will completely disconnect G from I.
    This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Verify minimality using Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. Let's find edge-disjoint paths from G to I:
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        Edges: {(G, H), (H, I)}
    *   **Path 2:** G - E - I
        Edges: {(G, E), (E, I)}
        (Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint as they share no common edges.)
    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - M - J - I
        Edges: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)}
        Let's check if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
        *   Path 3 vs Path 1: No common edges.
        *   Path 3 vs Path 2: No common edges. (G,E) is not (G,F) or (F,E). (E,I) is not (F,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,I).
        Therefore, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    We found a cut set of 3 edges: [(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)].
    We also determined that at least 3 edges are required to disconnect G from I because there are 3 edge-disjoint paths.
    Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}
Progress: 3/280 images processed.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from B.

1.  **Initial Assessment (Upper Bound):** A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges incident to it.
    *   Node H has three incident edges: (H, I), (H, G), and (H, X). If we remove these three edges, H becomes isolated, and thus disconnected from B. This gives an upper bound of 3 edges.
    *   Node B has four incident edges: (B, C), (B, N), (B, O), and (B, E). Removing these four edges would isolate B. This gives an upper bound of 4 edges.
    *   Since 3 is less than 4, the current best upper bound is 3.

2.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut (Lower Bound - Menger's Theorem):** To determine if a cut of size 1 or 2 is possible, I will try to find edge-disjoint paths between H and B. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (the min-cut) must be at least *k*.

    Let's search for edge-disjoint paths from H to B:

    *   **Path 1 (P1): H-I-J-P-O-B**
        Edges: {(H,I), (I,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,B)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): H-G-F-E-B**
        Edges: {(H,G), (G,F), (F,E), (E,B)}

    *   **Path 3 (P3): H-X-K-G-I-C-B**
        Edges: {(H,X), (X,K), (K,G), (G,I), (I,C), (C,B)}

    Now, let's verify if these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges in P1: (H,I), (I,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,B)
    *   Edges in P2: (H,G), (G,F), (F,E), (E,B)
    *   Edges in P3: (H,X), (X,K), (K,G), (G,I), (I,C), (C,B)

    By inspecting the lists, all edges in P1 are distinct from all edges in P2 and P3. Similarly, all edges in P2 are distinct from P1 and P3, and all edges in P3 are distinct from P1 and P2. For example:
    *   (H,I) is only in P1.
    *   (H,G) is only in P2.
    *   (H,X) is only in P3.
    *   (I,J) is only in P1.
    *   (G,F) is only in P2.
    *   (X,K) is only in P3.
    *   (J,P) is only in P1.
    *   (F,E) is only in P2.
    *   (K,G) is only in P3.
    *   (P,O) is only in P1.
    *   (E,B) is only in P2.
    *   (G,I) is only in P3.
    *   (O,B) is only in P1.
    *   (I,C) is only in P3.
    *   (C,B) is only in P3.

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

3.  **Conclusion:**
    *   We established an upper bound of 3 edges by isolating node H (removing (H,I), (H,G), (H,X)).
    *   We established a lower bound of 3 edges by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths.
    *   Since the upper bound and lower bound are both 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    The cut set [(H, I), (H, G), (H, X)] effectively disconnects H from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.

The final answer is 3 edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (H, G), (H, X)]

    *   Since there are at least two node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect H and O is at least 2.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from H.

1.  **Identify direct connections to H:**
    Node H is directly connected to B, G, and I. This means any path from B to H must either use the direct edge (B, H), or pass through G, or pass through I.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, I can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between B and H (Menger's Theorem).
    Let's identify some edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H (direct edge)
    *   **Path 2:** B - E - F - G - H
        *   Edges: (B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 as it does not use (B, H).
    *   **Path 3:** B - C - I - H
        *   Edges: (B, C), (C, I), (I, H)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (does not use (B, H)).
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 2:
            *   (B, C) is distinct from (B, E).
            *   (C, I) is distinct from (E, F), (F, G).
            *   (I, H) is distinct from (G, H).

    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect B and H is at least 3.

3.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    Since we know at least 3 edges are needed, I will try to find a cut set of exactly 3 edges.
    Consider the edges directly connected to H: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
    *   If I remove **(B, H)**, the direct path between B and H is broken.
    *   If I remove **(G, H)**, all paths from B to H that pass through G are broken (e.g., B-E-F-G-H, B-N-E-F-G-H).
    *   If I remove **(I, H)**, all paths from B to H that pass through I are broken (e.g., B-C-I-H, B-N-C-I-H, B-E-M-J-D-P-I-H).

    By removing these three edges [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)], node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from B. This set of 3 edges successfully disconnects B and H.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects B and H, this cut set is minimal.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

        *   Paths from N to H include: N-B-H, N-C-H, N-C-I-H, N-O-C-H, N-O-C-I-H, N-O-P-D-J-I-H, and various longer paths that eventually lead to B, C, or I.
        *   If node H is removed, all paths in this category are severed.

    *   **Category 2: Paths ending in ... -> K -> G or ... -> X -> G**
        *   Nodes K and X are connected to G, F, and each other.
        *   Therefore, any path from N to K or X (and then to G) must pass through node F.
        *   Node F is connected to E, K, X.
        *   Therefore, any path from N to F must pass through node E.
        *   Node E is connected to B, F, L.
        *   This means that all paths from N that eventually reach K or X (and then G) *must* pass through node E.
        *   If node E is removed, all paths in this category (to K or X) are severed.

5.  **Formulating a Cut Set:**
    Based on the analysis above:
    *   Removing node H will disconnect all paths that go directly to G via H.
    *   Removing node E will disconnect all paths that go to G via K or X (because they all must pass through E to reach F, which then connects to K or X).

    Therefore, removing both H and E should disconnect N from G. This is a cut set of size 2.

6.  **Verification of Cut Set [H, E]:**
    Let's confirm that no path remains between N and G if H and E are removed.
    *   Any path from N to G must use either H, K, or X as its last hop before G.
    *   If H is removed, all paths N-...-H-G are broken.
    *   If E is removed, all paths N-...-E-F-{K,X}-G are broken.
    *   Since all paths to G must go through H, K, or X, and removing H and E covers all these possibilities, N and G are completely disconnected.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    A single node removal is not sufficient. A set of two nodes, [H, E], successfully disconnects N from G. This is the minimum number of nodes.

The final answer is: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices (a vertex cut) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. Two paths are vertex-disjoint if they do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than E and I).

1.  **Path 1: E - M - J - I**
    Intermediate nodes: {M, J}

2.  **Path 2: E - G - H - I**
    Intermediate nodes: {G, H}
    This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as {G, H} does not overlap with {M, J}.

3.  **Path 3: E - B - C - I**
    Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
    This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, as {B, C} does not overlap with {M, J} or {G, H}.

4.  **Path 4: E - N - O - I**
    Intermediate nodes: {N, O}
    This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3, as {N, O} does not overlap with {M, J}, {G, H}, or {B, C}.

I have found 4 vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and I is at least 4.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 4 nodes and verify that its removal disconnects E and I. A straightforward approach is to remove one intermediate node from each of these 4 disjoint paths. For example, I can choose the first intermediate node from E for each path: M, G, B, N.

Let's verify that removing the nodes [M, G, B, N] disconnects E from I:
1.  **Remove M:** This breaks Path 1 (E-M-J-I) and any other path starting E-M-....
    After removing M, E's neighbor L is now isolated from the rest of the graph (L is only connected to E and M). So, E-L is a dead end.
2.  **Remove G:** This breaks Path 2 (E-G-H-I) and any other path starting E-G-....
3.  **Remove B:** This breaks Path 3 (E-B-C-I) and any other path starting E-B-....
4.  **Remove N:** This breaks Path 4 (E-N-O-I) and any other path starting E-N-....

After removing M, G, B, N:
-   E's direct neighbors are L, F.
-   L is only connected to E (M is removed). So E-L leads nowhere.
-   F is connected to E, X, K. (M and G are removed).
    -   From F, E can reach X or K.
    -   X is connected to F, K. (G is removed).
    -   K is connected to F, X. (G is removed).
-   So, after removing M, G, B, N, E can only reach nodes F, X, K.
-   Now, I check if any of F, X, K are connected to I or any of I's remaining neighbors (H, J, P, C, O).
    -   F is not connected to H, J, P, C, O.
    -   X is not connected to H, J, P, C, O.
    -   K is not connected to H, J, P, C, O.

Since E can no longer reach any of I's neighbors, E is completely disconnected from I.
The number of nodes removed is 4. Since I found 4 vertex-disjoint paths, 4 is the minimum number of nodes required.

    *   **N-side nodes:** N, Y, O, C, B, E, L, M, J, D, P, I.
    *   **G-side nodes:** G, H, K, F, X.

    *   Edge (P, F): Node P is on the N-side, Node F is on the G-side.
    *   Edge (I, H): Node I is on the N-side, Node H is on the G-side.

    Let's verify if there are any other edges crossing this partition:
    *   Nodes directly connected to N: Y, O, C, B (all on N-side).
    *   Nodes directly connected to G: H, K, F, X (all on G-side).
    *   Nodes connected to H (from outside G-side): I, C (both on N-side).
    *   Nodes connected to F (from outside G-side): P (on N-side).
    *   Nodes connected to K (from outside G-side): F, X (both on G-side).
    *   Nodes connected to X (from outside G-side): F, K (both on G-side).

    Therefore, the only edges connecting the N-side to the G-side are (P, F), (I, H), and (C, H).

    *   Cut Set: [(P, F), (I, H), (C, H)]
    *   Size of this cut set: 3.

5.  **Verify Minimality (Attempt to find a smaller cut):** To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that removing any 2 edges is not sufficient to disconnect N from G. This is equivalent to finding 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and G (Menger's Theorem). However, finding edge-disjoint paths can be complex. A more direct approach for small numbers is to test all combinations of 2 edges from the identified cut set.

    Let's try removing any two edges from the cut set [(P, F), (I, H), (C, H)] and see if a path still exists:

    *   **Case 1: Remove (P, F) and (I, H)**
        *   Path N - C - H - G still exists. (Edges (C,H) and (H,G) are intact).
        *   Therefore, N is still connected to G.

    *   **Case 2: Remove (P, F) and (C, H)**
        *   Path N - O - P - I - H - G still exists. (Edges (P,I), (I,H), (H,G) are intact).
        *   Path N - B - E - P - I - H - G still exists. (Edges (B,E), (E,P), (P,I), (I,H), (H,G) are intact).
        *   Therefore, N is still connected to G.

    *   **Case 3: Remove (I, H) and (C, H)**
        *   Path N - O - P - F - G still exists. (Edges (O,P), (P,F), (F,G) are intact).
        *   Path N - B - E - P - F - G still exists. (Edges (B,E), (E,P), (P,F), (F,G) are intact).
        *   Therefore, N is still connected to G.

    Since removing any combination of 2 edges from the identified cut set does not disconnect N from G, the minimum number of edges to remove must be 3.

    The edges connected to I are (H, I), (P, I), (J, I), and (C, I).

    *   **Is (C, I) part of any path from G to I?**
        Let's check if node C is reachable from G without passing through I.
        *   From G, I can go to H, K, F, X.
        *   H is not connected to C.
        *   K is not connected to C.
        *   F is not connected to C.
        *   X is not connected to C.
        *   P is not connected to C.
        *   J is not connected to C.
        *   N is connected to C, but N is not reachable from G without passing through I.
        *   O is connected to C, but O is not reachable from G without passing through I.
        Since C is not reachable from G without first passing through I, the edge (C, I) is not part of any path from G to I. Therefore, removing (C, I) is not necessary to disconnect G from I.

    *   **Remaining critical edges connected to I:**
        The edges that are part of paths from G to I are (H, I), (P, I), and (J, I).

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    *   If I remove (H, I), Path 1 (G-H-I) is broken.
    *   If I remove (P, I), all paths that go directly from P to I (e.g., G-F-P-I, G-K-X-F-P-I, G-X-F-P-I) are broken.
    *   If I remove (J, I), all paths that go from J to I (e.g., G-F-P-J-I, G-K-X-F-P-J-I, G-X-F-P-J-I) are broken.

    Let's test if a cut of size 1 or 2 is sufficient:
    *   **Size 1:** Removing any single edge (H, I), (P, I), or (J, I) would leave other paths intact. For example, removing (H, I) leaves paths through P and J. So, 1 edge is not enough.
    *   **Size 2:**
        *   Removing [(H, I), (P, I)]: Paths through J (e.g., G-F-P-J-I) would still exist.
        *   Removing [(H, I), (J, I)]: Paths through P directly to I (e.g., G-F-P-I) would still exist.
        *   Removing [(P, I), (J, I)]: Paths through H (G-H-I) would still exist.
        Therefore, 2 edges are not enough.

    *   **Size 3:**
        Consider the cut set: [(H, I), (P, I), (J, I)].
        *   Removing (H, I) breaks G-H-I.
        *   Removing (P, I) breaks G-F-P-I, G-K-X-F-P-I, G-X-F-P-I.
        *   Removing (J, I) breaks G-F-P-J-I, G-K-X-F-P-J-I, G-X-F-P-J-I.
        All identified paths from G to I are broken by removing these three edges. Since we've shown that 2 edges are insufficient, 3 edges is the minimum.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (P, I), (J, I)]

    Similarly, any path from L to H must end by traversing an edge into H, which could be (G, H), (B, H), or (I, H).

3.  **Evaluate the possibility of disconnecting with 1 edge:**
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example, (L, M), L can still reach H via paths starting with (L, E) (e.g., L-E-B-H).
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example, (L, E), L can still reach H via paths starting with (L, M) (e.g., L-M-F-G-H).
    *   If we remove an edge not directly connected to L, for example, (G, H), L can still reach H via paths through B or I (e.g., L-E-B-H or L-M-J-I-H).
    *   Since L has two distinct outgoing edges (L, M) and (L, E), and H has three distinct incoming edges (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H), removing any single edge will always leave at least one path between L and H.
    *   Therefore, 1 edge is not sufficient to disconnect L and H. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Evaluate the possibility of disconnecting with 2 edges:**
    *   **Option A: Remove all edges directly connected to L.**
        If we remove both (L, M) and (L, E), node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. If L is isolated, no path can exist from L to H.
        This cut set is [(L, M), (L, E)] and has a size of 2. This successfully disconnects L from H.

    *   **Option B: Consider other combinations of 2 edges.**
        Let's try to find a cut set of 2 edges that are not directly connected to L or H.
        *   Consider cutting edges that act as "bridges" further down the paths.
        *   Paths through M eventually go through F or J. Paths through E eventually go through B.
        *   Paths through F lead to G (e.g., L-M-F-G-H).
        *   Paths through J lead to I (e.g., L-M-J-I-H).
        *   Paths through B lead to H or I (e.g., L-E-B-H or L-E-B-N-C-I-H).
        *   If we try to cut (F, G) and (E, B):
            *   (F, G) cuts paths like L-M-F-G-H.
            *   (E, B) cuts paths like L-E-B-H and L-E-B-N-C-I-H.

        *   If we try to cut (H, G) and (H, B):
            *   Paths like L-M-J-I-H would still exist. So, this is not enough.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 1 edge is not sufficient, and we have found a set of 2 edges (L, M) and (L, E) that successfully disconnects L from H, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

3.  **Explore paths and identify bottlenecks:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

    Let's find vertex-disjoint paths between H and N (excluding H and N themselves):

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        *   Intermediate node: {B}

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - K - J - D - P - O - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, K, J, D, P, O}
        *   Check for disjointness with Path 1 and Path 2:
            *   G, K, J, D, P, O are all distinct from B.
            *   G, K, J, D, P, O are all distinct from I and C.
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

    Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, J and F. I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut (min-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths (max-flow) between the two nodes.

First, I will identify a set of edge-disjoint paths from J to F.
1.  **Path 1: J - M - L - F**
    Edges: (J, M), (M, L), (L, F)

2.  **Path 2: J - D - P - O - B - F**
    Edges: (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, B), (B, F)

3.  **Path 3: J - P - Y - N - B - E - F**
    Edges: (J, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, E), (E, F)

4.  **Path 4: J - I - H - G - F**
    Edges: (J, I), (I, H), (H, G), (G, F)

Now, I will verify if these four paths are indeed edge-disjoint by listing all edges used and checking for overlaps:
*   Edges from Path 1: (J, M), (M, L), (L, F)
*   Edges from Path 2: (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, B), (B, F)
*   Edges from Path 3: (J, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, E), (E, F)
*   Edges from Path 4: (J, I), (I, H), (H, G), (G, F)

Upon inspection, all edges listed are unique to their respective paths. There are no common edges between any pair of these four paths. Therefore, these four paths are edge-disjoint.

According to Menger's Theorem, since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect J and F is at least 4.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that successfully disconnects J from F. I will choose one edge from each of the identified edge-disjoint paths, ensuring that these chosen edges form a minimal cut.

Consider the following cut set:
*   **(M, L)**: This edge cuts Path 1 (J - M - L - F).
*   **(B, F)**: This edge cuts Path 2 (J - D - P - O - B - F) and all other paths that directly connect to F via B.
*   **(B, E)**: This edge cuts Path 3 (J - P - Y - N - B - E - F) and all other paths that connect to F via B then E.
*   **(I, H)**: This edge cuts Path 4 (J - I - H - G - F) and all other paths that go through I to H (e.g., J-I-H-X-K-F, J-I-H-C-B-F).

Let's verify that removing these four edges completely disconnects J from F:
1.  **Paths through L to F**:
    *   The direct path J-M-L-F is cut by (M, L).
    *   Any path to L must come from M (J-M-L) or E (J-...-E-L). If it comes from E, it must be J-...-B-E-L. This path is cut by (B, E). Thus, all paths to L are cut.
2.  **Paths through E to F**:
    *   Any path to E must come from B (J-...-B-E-F) or L (J-...-L-E-F).
    *   Paths J-...-B-E-F are cut by (B, E).
    *   Paths J-...-L-E-F (e.g., J-M-L-E-F) are cut by (M, L). Thus, all paths to E are cut.
3.  **Paths through B to F**:
    *   Paths that go directly J-...-B-F are cut by (B, F).
    *   Paths that go J-...-B-E-F are cut by (B, E). Thus, all paths to B are cut.
4.  **Paths through K to F**:
    *   All paths to K from J must pass through I-H (e.g., J-I-H-X-K-F, J-I-H-G-K-F). These are cut by (I, H). Thus, all paths to K are cut.
5.  **Paths through G to F**:
    *   All paths to G from J must pass through I-H (e.g., J-I-H-G-F, J-I-H-X-G-F). These are cut by (I, H). Thus, all paths to G are cut.

Since all possible routes to F (via L, E, B, K, G) are severed by the removal of these four edges, J and F are completely disconnected.

The number of edges in this cut set is 4, which matches the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths found. Therefore, this is the minimum number of edges to remove.

First, I identify all edges directly connected to node M. These are the "entry points" to M from the rest of the graph.
The edges connected to M are:
1. (E, M)
2. (J, M)
3. (L, M)

To completely disconnect M from the rest of the graph, and thus from B, all paths leading to M must be severed. Since M has a degree of 3 (it is connected to E, J, and L), any path to M must use one of these three edges.

Let's consider the number of edges required:
- If I remove only one edge (e.g., (E, M)), paths through (J, M) and (L, M) would still exist, connecting M to the graph. For example, B-I-J-M or B-E-L-M.
- If I remove any two edges (e.g., (E, M) and (J, M)), the remaining edge (L, M) would still connect M to the graph. For example, the path B-E-L-M would still exist.
- Therefore, to ensure no path exists between B and M, all three edges directly connected to M must be removed.

Removing the edges (E, M), (J, M), and (L, M) will isolate M from the entire graph, guaranteeing that no path can exist between M and B. This set of three edges is a minimal cut set because removing any fewer edges would leave at least one path to M.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (J, M), (L, M)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and H. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and H. I cannot remove E or H.

2.  **Identify Direct Connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to B, G, and I. Any path from E to H must pass through at least one of these three nodes (B, G, I) as its last step before reaching H.

3.  **Explore Paths and Potential Bottlenecks:**
    Let's consider the "channels" through which paths flow from E to H.

    *   **Channel 1: Through B**
        *   There is a direct path: E - B - H.
        *   Other paths through B: E - B - N - C - I - H, E - B - N - O - P - I - H, E - B - C - I - H, etc.
        *   Node B is a critical point for all paths that use it.

    *   **Channel 2: Through G**
        *   Paths to G: E - F - K - G - H, E - F - X - K - G - H, E - L - F - K - G - H, E - M - L - F - K - G - H, etc.
        *   Notice that G is only connected to K and H. Therefore, any path from E to G (and subsequently to H) *must* pass through node K. If K is removed, all paths through G are severed. So, K is a critical point for this channel.

    *   **Channel 3: Through I**
        *   Paths to I: E - M - J - I - H, E - M - J - D - P - I - H, E - M - J - D - P - O - N - C - I - H, etc.
        *   Paths to I can also come from B (e.g., E - B - C - I - H).

4.  **Hypothesize a Cut Set and Verify:**
    Based on the analysis of the three channels, it appears that we need to address B, K (for G), and J (for I, assuming B is handled). Let's propose the cut set **[B, K, J]**.

    *   **Remove B:** This immediately breaks the direct path E - B - H and all other paths that utilize B (e.g., E - B - N - C - I - H). Now, E can only reach H via G or I.
    *   **Remove K:** With K removed, G is now only connected to H. All paths from E to G (e.g., E - F - K - G - H) are broken because they all pass through K. So, H cannot be reached via G.
    *   **Remove J:** With J removed, and B already removed, let's check paths from E to I. I is connected to P, C, H, J. Since B is removed, paths from E to I cannot use B, C, or N. All remaining paths from E to I must go through M, then J (e.g., E - M - J - I - H, E - M - J - D - P - I - H). Since J is removed, all these paths are broken. So, H cannot be reached via I.

    Therefore, removing nodes B, K, and J successfully disconnects E from H. This is a cut set of size 3.

5.  **Prove Minimum Size (Menger's Theorem):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I will look for three vertex-disjoint paths between E and H (paths that share no intermediate nodes).

    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H (Intermediate node: {B})
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - G - H (Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G})
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - I - H (Intermediate nodes: {M, J, I})

    Let's check for shared intermediate nodes:
    *   {B} is distinct from {F, K, G}.
    *   {B} is distinct from {M, J, I}.
    *   {F, K, G} is distinct from {M, J, I}.

    Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between E and H, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E and H is at least 3.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut set of size 3 ([B, K, J]) has been found, and it has been proven that at least 3 nodes are required, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, K, J]

A more efficient way to isolate E from the "J-side" of the graph would be to cut the edges connecting E to M, H, and L, as F and G only lead to H. So, removing (E, M), (E, H), (E, L) would disconnect E from J. This is a cut set of size 3.

    *   Another primary channel involves paths starting from E and going through H (E-H-...). All these paths rely on the edge (E, H) to leave E towards H. Paths starting E-F-G-H-... or E-G-H-... also rely on H, and thus on (E,H) if E is the source.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 9
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B (black) and M (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B and M.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** B and M.

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. I will try to find edge-disjoint paths between B and M.

3.  **Find edge-disjoint paths:**
    Let's identify paths from B to M and ensure they do not share any edges.

    *   **Path 1:** B -> I -> E -> M
        Edges: `(B, I)`, `(I, E)`, `(E, M)`

    *   **Path 2:** B -> H -> E -> L -> M
        Edges: `(B, H)`, `(H, E)`, `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`
        *   Check for disjointness with Path 1:
            *   `(B, I)` vs `(B, H)`: Disjoint.
            *   `(I, E)` vs `(H, E)`: Disjoint.
            *   `(E, M)` vs `(E, L)`: Disjoint.
            *   `(L, M)` is unique to Path 2.
        *   Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** B -> C -> N -> O -> P -> J -> M
        Edges: `(B, C)`, `(C, N)`, `(N, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, J)`, `(J, M)`
        *   Check for disjointness with Path 1:
            *   `(B, I)` vs `(B, C)`: Disjoint.
            *   No other common edges. Path 1 and Path 3 are edge-disjoint.
        *   Check for disjointness with Path 2:
            *   `(B, H)` vs `(B, C)`: Disjoint.
            *   No other common edges. Path 2 and Path 3 are edge-disjoint.

    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect B and M.

4.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    To confirm that 3 is indeed the minimum, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects B and M. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, if that number is small.

    Node M has three direct connections:
    *   `(E, M)`
    *   `(J, M)`
    *   `(L, M)`

    If these three edges are removed, node M becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Therefore, no path can exist between B and M. This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths (implying a minimum cut of at least 3) and I found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

First, I will identify vertex-disjoint paths from G to I. Vertex-disjoint paths share no common nodes other than the start (G) and end (I) nodes.

1.  **Path 1: G - H - I**

G's remaining direct neighbors: {F, X}
I's remaining direct neighbors: {P, C}

3.  **Path 3: G - F - M - L - E - B - C - I**

I's remaining direct neighbors: {P}

-   X is connected to F (used in P3) and K (used in P2).
    Since both of X's neighbors are already used in previous disjoint paths, X cannot initiate a new, fourth vertex-disjoint path.

Therefore, I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 nodes that disconnects G from I. To do this, I need to select one node from each of the identified disjoint paths (excluding G and I).

From Path 1 (G-H-I): I can choose H.
From Path 2 (G-K-J-I): I can choose K or J. Let's choose J.
From Path 3 (G-F-M-L-E-B-C-I): I can choose F, M, L, E, B, or C.

-   Removing J breaks G-K-J-I and all paths through K-J.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, M and B. This is a classic minimum cut problem in graph theory. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B.

2.  **Explore paths from M to B:** I will systematically look for paths starting from M and ending at B.

    *   Edges in Path 1: (M, O), (O, B)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (M, L), (L, E), (E, B)

    *   The edge (M, O) is unique to Path 1.
    *   The edge (O, B) is unique to Path 1 among these three paths.
    *   The edge (M, J) is unique to Path 2.
    *   The edges (J, I), (I, C), (C, B) are unique to Path 2 among these three paths.
    *   The edge (M, L) is unique to Path 3.
    *   The edges (L, E), (E, B) are unique to Path 3 among these three paths.

    Since these three paths (M-O-B, M-J-I-C-B, M-L-E-B) do not share any common edges, they are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Because I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect M from B is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect M from B is to remove all edges directly connected to M. These edges are (M, J), (M, O), and (M, L).
    If these three edges are removed, M becomes an isolated node, and therefore no path can exist between M and B. This cut set has a size of 3.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum cut must be at least 3 (due to the 3 edge-disjoint paths) and I have found a valid cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

        *   G -> H -> I -> C -> N

    *   Any path from G that goes through E (e.g., G-E-M-J-I-C-N, G-E-M-J-P-O-N, or even G-E-H-B-N) would be broken if E is removed.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify potential "entry points" to J from the rest of the graph:**
    Any path from E to J must eventually reach one of J's neighbors (excluding D, as D is only connected to J and no other node that E is connected to, meaning no path from E can reach D and then J). The relevant neighbors of J are M, K, I, and P.

2.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths between E and J:**
    According to Menger's Theorem (edge version), the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible:

    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`

    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - J
        Edges: `(E, F)`, `(F, K)`, `(K, J)`

    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
        Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`, `(H, I)`, `(I, J)`

    *   **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, J)`

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's list the edges for each path and check for overlaps:
    *   P1 edges: `{(E, M), (M, J)}`
    *   P2 edges: `{(E, F), (F, K), (K, J)}`
    *   P3 edges: `{(E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)}`
    *   P4 edges: `{(E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)}`

    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between any of these four paths. Therefore, we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and J.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
    Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and J is 4.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 4:**
    A straightforward way to cut all these paths is to remove the edges directly connecting to J from the "E-side" of the graph. These edges are:
    *   `(M, J)` (cuts Path 1 and any other path through M to J, like E-L-M-J)
    *   `(K, J)` (cuts Path 2 and any other path through K to J, like E-F-X-K-J, E-G-F-K-J, E-G-F-X-K-J)
    *   `(I, J)` (cuts Path 3 and any other path through I to J, like E-B-C-I-J, E-B-N-C-I-J)
    *   `(P, J)` (cuts Path 4 and any other path through P to J, like E-B-N-C-O-P-J, E-B-N-Y-O-P-J)

6.  **Verify the cut set:**
    Removing these four edges ensures that J is isolated from M, K, I, and P. Since all paths from E to J must pass through one of these nodes (M, K, I, P), removing these edges guarantees that no path exists between E and J. The node D is only connected to J and not to any other node that E is connected to, so the edge (D, J) is not part of any path from E to J and does not need to be removed.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

    *   If we remove (N,B), paths like H-I-C-N, H-I-P-O-N, H-G-K-J-I-C-N, etc., still exist.
    *   No single edge removal can disconnect H and N. Therefore, the minimum number of edges is greater than 1.

3.  **Consider a cut of size 2:**

    *   **Attempt 1: Cut edges directly connected to H.**
        If we remove (H,B) and (H,I), H can still reach N via G. For example, H-G-E-B-N, H-G-K-J-I-C-N, H-G-K-J-P-O-N. These paths are still intact. So, 2 edges are not enough here.
    *   **Attempt 2: Cut edges directly connected to N.**
        If we remove (N,B) and (N,C):
        *   Paths like H-B-N and H-G-E-B-N are cut by (N,B).
        *   Paths like H-I-C-N and H-G-K-J-I-C-N are cut by (N,C).
        *   However, paths that go through O to N are still connected, e.g., H-I-P-O-N, H-G-E-B-O-N, H-G-K-J-P-O-N. These paths use the edge (O,N), which is not removed. So, 2 edges are not enough here.
    *   **Attempt 3: Cut a mix of edges.**

        *   H-B-N is cut by (H,B).
        *   H-I-C-N is cut by (N,C).
        *   However, paths like H-I-P-O-N, H-G-E-B-N, H-G-E-B-O-N, H-G-K-J-P-O-N still exist. So, 2 edges are not enough.

    Based on these attempts, it appears that 2 edges are not sufficient to disconnect H and N. Therefore, the minimum number of edges is greater than 2.

4.  **Consider a cut of size 3:**
    *   **Option A: Isolate H by removing all its outgoing edges.**
        The edges directly connected to H are (H,G), (H,B), and (H,I). If these three edges are removed, H becomes an isolated node and cannot reach N.
        Cut Set: [(H,G), (H,B), (H,I)]
        This is a valid cut set of size 3.

    *   **Option B: Isolate N from its main connections to the rest of the graph.**
        Node N is connected to B, C, Y, and O. Let's consider removing the edges (N,B), (N,C), and (N,O).
        *   Removing (N,B) breaks all paths that reach N via node B (e.g., H-B-N, H-G-E-B-N).
        *   Removing (N,C) breaks all paths that reach N via node C (e.g., H-I-C-N, H-G-K-J-I-C-N).
        *   Removing (N,O) breaks all paths that reach N via node O (e.g., H-I-P-O-N, H-G-K-J-P-O-N).
        *   What about the connection (N,Y)? Node Y is only connected to N and O. Any path from H to Y must pass through O (e.g., H-B-O-Y, H-I-P-O-Y, H-G-E-B-O-Y, H-G-K-J-P-O-Y). Since (N,O) is removed, even if H reaches Y, Y cannot reach N via O. Therefore, any path H-...-Y-N would be broken because its connection to N via Y would ultimately rely on O, which is disconnected from N.
        This set [(N,B), (N,C), (N,O)] is a valid cut set of size 3.

Since we have found valid cut sets of size 3, and we have determined that no cut set of size 1 or 2 exists, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)] or [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and H. Our goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E from H.

2.  **Analyze Connectivity and Paths:**
    Let's list the direct neighbors of E and H:
    *   Neighbors of E: F, G, H, L, M
    *   Neighbors of H: E, F, G, B, I

    We can categorize the paths from E to H into three main groups:
    *   **Direct Path:** E - H
    *   **Left-Side Paths (via F or G):** Paths that go from E to F or G, and then from F or G to H. Examples: E-F-H, E-G-H, E-F-G-H.
    *   **Right-Side Paths (via L/M and I/B):** Paths that go from E to L or M, then through the right cluster (J, P, D, I, B, C, O, N, Y) to eventually reach I or B, and finally to H. Examples: E-L-M-J-I-H, E-M-J-P-O-C-B-H.

3.  **Find Edge-Disjoint Paths (Lower Bound for Min-Cut):**
    To determine the minimum number of edges, we can try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. By Menger's Theorem, this number equals the minimum number of edges in a cut set.

    Let's identify four edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - H (Edges: (E,H))
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - H (Edges: (E,F), (F,H))
    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H (Edges: (E,G), (G,H))
    *   **Path 4:** E - L - M - J - I - H (Edges: (E,L), (L,M), (M,J), (J,I), (I,H))

    Let's verify that these four paths are indeed edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   P1: (E,H)
    *   P2: (E,F), (F,H)
    *   P3: (E,G), (G,H)
    *   P4: (E,L), (L,M), (M,J), (J,I), (I,H)
    All edges in these four paths are unique. Therefore, there are at least 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove (the min-cut) must be at least 4.

4.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 4:**
    Now that we know the minimum is at least 4, let's try to find a set of 4 edges whose removal disconnects E and H.
    Consider the following set of edges:
    **Cut Set: [(E,H), (F,H), (G,H), (M,J)]**

    Let's verify if removing these 4 edges disconnects E from H:
    *   **Removing (E,H):** This breaks the direct path between E and H.
    *   **Removing (F,H) and (G,H):**
        *   Any path from E to H that goes through F (e.g., E-F-H, E-F-G-H, E-K-F-H) will be broken by the removal of (F,H).
        *   Any path from E to H that goes through G (e.g., E-G-H, E-G-F-H, E-X-G-H) will be broken by the removal of (G,H).
        *   Thus, all paths from E to H that utilize the "left" cluster (F, G, K, X) are severed.
    *   **Removing (M,J):**
        *   From E, paths to the "right" cluster can go to L or M.
        *   From L, the only way to proceed towards H is via M (L-M).
        *   From M, the only way to proceed towards H (via I or B) is through J (M-J).
        *   Since (M,J) is removed, E can no longer reach J, and consequently cannot reach I or B (which are connected to J or its descendants). Therefore, all paths from E to H that utilize the "right" cluster are severed.

    Since all three categories of paths (direct, left-side, right-side) are broken by the removal of these 4 edges, E and H are completely disconnected.

5.  **Verify Minimality:**
    To confirm that this cut set is minimal, we must ensure that removing any single edge from the set would leave E and H connected.
    *   If (E,H) is kept, the direct path E-H remains.
    *   If (F,H) is kept, the path E-F-H remains (assuming (E,H), (G,H), (M,J) are removed).
    *   If (G,H) is kept, the path E-G-H remains (assuming (E,H), (F,H), (M,J) are removed).
    *   If (M,J) is kept, the path E-L-M-J-I-H remains (assuming (E,H), (F,H), (G,H) are removed).
    Since each edge is essential, the cut set is minimal.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

    *   Node G is connected to X, K, F, H.
    *   Node N is connected to B, C, O, Y.

2.  **Analyze paths to N's immediate neighbors:**
    Any path from G to N must pass through one of N's direct neighbors: B, C, O, or Y.
    *   Node Y is a leaf node connected only to N. This means any path from G to N cannot use Y as an intermediate node to reach N; Y is a dead-end for paths *to* N. Therefore, the edge (Y, N) does not need to be cut to disconnect G from N.
    *   The relevant direct connections to N are (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N).

3.  **Consider a cut by removing edges directly connected to N:**
    If we remove the edges (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N), then N will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph (except for Y, which is also isolated with N). This would ensure no path exists from G to N. This is a cut of size 3.

4.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut (size 2):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that no set of 2 edges can disconnect G from N. This is equivalent to finding at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N (by Menger's Theorem).

    Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1 (to B):** G - H - B - N
        Edges: {(G, H), (H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2 (to C):** G - F - E - I - C - N
        Edges: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, C), (C, N)}

    *   **Path 3 (to O):** We need a path from G to O that is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
        Edges used by Path 1: (G, H), (H, B), (B, N)
        Edges used by Path 2: (G, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, C), (C, N)

        To construct Path 3, we must avoid these edges.
        Starting from G, we cannot use (G, H) or (G, F). So, Path 3 must start with (G, K) or (G, X).
        *   If we go G - K:
            *   G - K - X. Node X is connected to F, which is used by Path 2.
            *   G - K - F. Node F is used by Path 2.
        *   If we go G - X:
            *   G - X - K. Node K is connected to F, which is used by Path 2.
            *   G - X - F. Node F is used by Path 2.

        This indicates that any path from G that does not start with (G, H) or (G, F) must eventually pass through F (via K or X). Since F is already part of Path 2, it means that any third path from G to N would necessarily share edges with Path 2 (specifically, (G,F) or (F,E) or edges further along from F).

    This analysis suggests that all paths from G to N must pass through either H or F.
    Let's verify this:
    *   Paths starting G-H: These are covered by the 'H' branch.
    *   Paths starting G-F: These are covered by the 'F' branch.
    *   Paths starting G-K: Must go G-K-F or G-K-X-F. Both lead to F.
    *   Paths starting G-X: Must go G-X-F or G-X-K-F. Both lead to F.
    So, indeed, all paths from G to N must pass through either H or F.

    Now, let's consider the edges that connect H and F to the "N-side" of the graph.
    *   From H, edges are (H, B) and (H, I).
    *   From F, the edge is (F, E).

    To disconnect G from N, we need to cut all paths originating from H and all paths originating from F.
    Let's try to find a cut of size 2 by selecting edges from these sets:
    *   **Attempt 1: Remove (H, B) and (F, E).**
        *   Paths like G - H - I - C - N still exist.
        *   Paths like G - H - I - P - O - N still exist.
        *   Therefore, this is not a cut.

    *   **Attempt 2: Remove (H, I) and (F, E).**
        *   Paths like G - H - B - N still exist.
        *   Paths like G - H - B - C - N still exist.
        *   Therefore, this is not a cut.

    Since we cannot find a cut of size 2, and we have identified a cut of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

5.  **Final Cut Set:**
    The most straightforward and minimal cut is to remove the edges directly connecting to N from the rest of the graph (excluding the leaf node Y). These are (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N).

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

    After removing C, the direct connection H-C-N is broken. Now, I need to find all other paths from H to N and identify the minimum number of nodes to remove to break them.
    The remaining neighbors of H are I, X, G.
    The remaining neighbors of N are Y, O, B. (Y is only connected to N, so no paths from H can reach N via Y without passing through O or B first, which is not the case here).
    So, after removing C, I need to disconnect H from {O, B}.

4.  **Categorize Remaining Paths:** I can group the remaining paths from H to N based on their first step from H (after C is removed):

    *   **Group 1: Paths starting with H-I:**
        *   H - I - J - M - O - N
        *   H - I - J - D - P - O - N
        *   H - I - J - P - O - N

        *   H - X - K - F - E - B - N
        *   H - X - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
        *   H - G - X - K - F - E - B - N
        *   H - G - X - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
        *   H - G - K - F - E - B - N
        *   H - G - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
        All these paths go through node E. If E is removed, all paths in Group 2 are broken.

5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Set:**
    *   As established in step 2, C must be removed.
    *   After removing C, the paths in Group 1 (via I-J) and Group 2 (via X/G-K-F-E) represent two distinct sets of paths that do not share any common intermediate nodes (other than H and N themselves, which cannot be removed).
    *   To break all paths in Group 1, I must remove at least one node from that group. Node J is a single point that all paths in Group 1 pass through.
    *   To break all paths in Group 2, I must remove at least one node from that group. Node E is a single point that all paths in Group 2 pass through.
    *   Therefore, to disconnect H from N, I must remove C, J, and E. This gives a cut set of size 3.

6.  **Verify Minimality:**
    *   A cut set of size 1 is not possible because removing C alone leaves the paths in Group 1 and Group 2 intact.
    *   A cut set of size 2 is not possible:
        *   If C is removed, I need to break both Group 1 and Group 2 paths. Removing only J (e.g., [C, J]) would leave Group 2 paths intact. Removing only E (e.g., [C, E]) would leave Group 1 paths intact. Removing any other single node (e.g., O or B) would still leave other paths active (e.g., [C, O] leaves H-X-K-F-E-B-N, [C, B] leaves H-I-J-M-O-N).
    *   Since a cut set of size 1 or 2 is not sufficient, the minimum size must be 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and H. I cannot remove E or H.

First, I will identify all possible paths between E and H.
Let's list the direct neighbors of E: F, L, M, I, B.
Let's list the direct neighbors of H: G, B, I.

Now, I will trace all distinct paths from E to H:

1.  **E - F - G - H** (This path uses intermediate nodes F and G)
2.  **E - B - H** (This path uses intermediate node B)
3.  **E - I - H** (This path uses intermediate node I)

Let's explore paths starting from E's other neighbors (L, M) that eventually lead to H:

4.  **E - L - M - J - I - H** (This path uses intermediate nodes L, M, J, I)
5.  **E - M - J - I - H** (This path uses intermediate nodes M, J, I)
6.  **E - M - J - P - I - H** (This path uses intermediate nodes M, J, P, I)

Now, I need to find a minimum set of nodes (excluding E and H) whose removal disconnects all these paths. This is a minimum vertex cut problem. A key concept here is Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths (paths that share no intermediate nodes) between E and H:

*   **Path A:** E - F - G - H (Intermediate nodes: F, G)
*   **Path B:** E - B - H (Intermediate node: B)
*   **Path C:** E - I - H (Intermediate node: I)

These three paths (Path A, Path B, Path C) are vertex-disjoint because their intermediate nodes (F, G, B, I) are all distinct.
Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect E and H must be at least 3.

Now, let's verify if removing the intermediate nodes from these three paths (F, B, I) successfully disconnects E and H.

If nodes F, B, and I are removed:
*   Path 1 (E - F - G - H) is broken because F is removed.
*   Path 2 (E - B - H) is broken because B is removed.
*   Path 3 (E - I - H) is broken because I is removed.
*   Path 4 (E - L - M - J - I - H) is broken because I is removed.
*   Path 5 (E - M - J - I - H) is broken because I is removed.
*   Path 6 (E - M - J - P - I - H) is broken because I is removed.

All identified paths between E and H are broken by removing nodes F, B, and I. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a set of 3 nodes that works, this is the minimum cut set.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [F, B, I]

        *   Therefore, node B acts as a single point of failure for all paths originating from F and passing through E. If B is removed, all these paths are broken.

    *   All paths from F to J must either go through {K, X, G} or through E.
    *   If a path goes through {K, X, G}, it *must* then pass through H to continue towards J.
    *   If a path goes through E, it *must* then pass through B to continue towards J.

    *   Therefore, removing both H and B will sever all connections from F to J.

        *   Removing B breaks all paths F -> E -> B -> ... -> J.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G and I. The task is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect G and I. This is a minimum cut problem, which by Menger's theorem, is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are G and I.

2.  **Consider simple cut sets:**
    *   To isolate G, we could remove all edges connected to it: (G, E), (G, F), (G, H), (G, B). This is a cut set of size 4.
    *   To isolate I, we could remove all edges connected to it: (H, I), (J, I), (C, I), (P, I). This is also a cut set of size 4.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set is possible:**
    *   A cut set of size 1 is not possible, as G and I are clearly connected by multiple distinct paths.
    *   Let's try to find if a cut set of size 2 or 3 exists. This requires systematically exploring paths.

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:** To prove that 4 is the minimum, we need to find 4 paths from G to I that do not share any common edges.

    Let's identify potential edge-disjoint paths:

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        *   Edges: (G, H), (H, I)

    *   **Path 2:** G - B - C - I
        *   Edges: (G, B), (B, C), (C, I)

    *   **Path 3:** G - E - L - M - J - I
        *   Edges: (G, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I)

    *   **Path 4:** G - F - K - J - P - I
        *   Edges: (G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, I)

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    Let's list all edges used in these four paths and check for any overlaps:
    *   From Path 1: (G, H), (H, I)
    *   From Path 2: (G, B), (B, C), (C, I)
    *   From Path 3: (G, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I)
    *   From Path 4: (G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, I)

    *   **Starting edges from G:** (G, H), (G, B), (G, E), (G, F) are all distinct.
    *   **Ending edges to I:** (H, I), (C, I), (J, I), (P, I) are all distinct.
    *   **Intermediate edges:**
        *   (B, C) is unique to Path 2.
        *   (E, L), (L, M), (M, J) are unique to Path 3.
        *   (F, K), (K, J), (J, P) are unique to Path 4.
    
    All edges listed are distinct. Therefore, these four paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

6.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is at least 4.
    We also identified a cut set of size 4 by removing all edges directly connected to I: [(H, I), (J, I), (C, I), (P, I)]. Removing these edges isolates I, ensuring no path exists from G to I.

    Since we found a cut set of size 4 and proved that the minimum cut must be at least 4, the minimum number of edges to remove is exactly 4.

Since no single node removal is sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

    *   **Can it be 2 edges?** Let's assume a cut set of 2 edges, {e1, e2}, exists.
        *   The direct path (H, B) must be broken. If (H, B) is not removed, H and B remain connected. So, one of the edges, say e1, must be (H, B).
        *   Now, with (H, B) removed, the second edge e2 must break *all* remaining paths. These paths branch out from H through G or I, and eventually reach B.
            *   Paths through G: H - G - ... - E - B
            *   Paths through I: H - I - ... - C - B and H - I - ... - N - B
        *   Can a single edge e2 cut all these remaining paths?
            *   If e2 is (H, G), paths through I still exist.
            *   If e2 is (H, I), paths through G still exist.
            *   If e2 is (E, B), paths through I still exist.
            *   If e2 is (C, B), paths through G and paths through I-P-O-N-B still exist.
            *   If e2 is (N, B), paths through G and paths through I-C-B still exist.
        *   Since no single edge e2 can cut all remaining paths, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

3.  **Search for a cut set of 3 edges.**
    *   Based on the analysis above, we know (H, B) must be removed.
    *   After removing (H, B), we need to cut the paths that go through G and the paths that go through I.
    *   To cut all paths originating from H through G, we can remove the edge (H, G). This breaks both H-G-F-E-B and H-G-X-F-E-B.
    *   To cut all paths originating from H through I, we can remove the edge (H, I). This breaks H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, and H-I-P-O-N-B.
    *   Therefore, the set of edges [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)] will disconnect H from B.

4.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    *   Removing (H, B) breaks the direct connection.
    *   Removing (H, G) ensures H cannot reach G, and thus cannot reach B via any path starting with H-G.
    *   Removing (H, I) ensures H cannot reach I, and thus cannot reach B via any path starting with H-I.
    *   With these three edges removed, H is completely isolated from B.

5.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and H.
2.  **Objective:** Determine the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect node E from node H.
3.  **Strategy (Menger's Theorem):** To find the minimum edge cut, I will look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. Menger's Theorem states that this number is equal to the minimum size of an edge cut.
4.  **Identify Edge-Disjoint Paths:** I will systematically search for paths from E to H that do not share any common edges.
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, H)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - G - H
        *   Edges: (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H)
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)
5.  **Verify Edge-Disjoint Property:** I check if any edges are shared between these three paths.
    *   Path 1 uses nodes E, B, H.
    *   Path 2 uses nodes E, F, K, G, H.
    *   Path 3 uses nodes E, M, J, P, I, H.
    *   The edges used in Path 1 are distinct from those in Path 2 and Path 3.
    *   The edges used in Path 2 are distinct from those in Path 1 and Path 3.
    *   The edges used in Path 3 are distinct from those in Path 1 and Path 2.
    *   Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.
6.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E from H.
7.  **Find a Minimal Cut Set:** Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E and H. A straightforward approach is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    *   Node H is connected to B, G, and I via edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
    *   If I remove these three edges:

    *   With these three edges removed, node H has no remaining connections to the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between E and H.

I will systematically explore possible cut sets, starting with the smallest possible size.

**Attempt 1: Cut Set of Size 1**

*   Removing (J, I): Paths like E-M-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, E-F-G-H-B-C-O-P-J still exist.

**Attempt 2: Cut Set of Size 2**

        *   Since all paths to G must pass through either H or F, removing both H and F will disconnect N and G. This set [H, F] also works.

        *   Consider paths from N to F: All paths from N to F must pass through P (as established in Option 1). So, removing P disconnects N from F.
        *   Since all paths from N to G must pass through H or F, removing C (to disconnect N from H) and P (to disconnect N from F) will disconnect N from G. This set [C, P] also works.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node cut is not possible, and we have found multiple cut sets of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, P] (or [H, F] or [C, P])

2.  **E - P - J**: This is a direct path through node P.

Node D is only connected to J and has no connection to E's side of the graph, so no path from E will go through D to J. Thus, any other path must eventually reach J via node I.

Let's trace paths from E to I, and then from I to J:
Paths from E to I:
    a. E - P - I
    b. E - B - C - I
    c. E - B - C - H - I
    d. E - P - F - G - H - I

4.  **E - B - C - I - J**: This path passes through B, C, and I.
5.  **E - B - C - H - I - J**: This path passes through B, C, H, and I.
6.  **E - P - F - G - H - I - J**: This path passes through P, F, G, H, and I.

Now, let's identify the critical nodes that must be removed to break these paths.
-   Path 1 (E - M - J) is uniquely broken by removing node M.
-   Paths 2, 3, and 6 (E - P - J, E - P - I - J, E - P - F - G - H - I - J) all start with E - P. Removing node P will break all these paths.
-   Paths 4 and 5 (E - B - C - I - J, E - B - C - H - I - J) both start with E - B. Removing node B will break both these paths.

Therefore, to disconnect E and J, we need to remove at least one node from each of these three "bottleneck" points: M, P, and B.

Let's test if removing these three nodes ([M, P, B]) disconnects E and J:
-   Removing M breaks E - M - J.
-   Removing P breaks E - P - J, E - P - I - J, and E - P - F - G - H - I - J.
-   Removing B breaks E - B - C - I - J and E - B - C - H - I - J.
Since all identified paths are broken by removing M, P, and B, this set of nodes successfully disconnects E and J. The size of this cut set is 3.

Now, I must verify that no smaller set of nodes can achieve disconnection.
-   **Can 1 node disconnect E and J?**
    -   Removing M leaves paths through P and B open.
    -   Removing P leaves paths through M and B open.
    -   Removing B leaves paths through M and P open.
    -   Removing I leaves paths through M and P open (E-M-J, E-P-J).
    -   No single node can disconnect E and J.

-   **Can 2 nodes disconnect E and J?**
    -   Removing [M, P]: Paths E-B-C-I-J and E-B-C-H-I-J remain open.
    -   Removing [M, B]: Paths E-P-J, E-P-I-J, and E-P-F-G-H-I-J remain open.
    -   Removing [P, B]: Path E-M-J remains open.
    -   Removing [M, I]: Path E-P-J remains open.
    -   Removing [P, I]: Path E-M-J remains open.
    -   Removing [B, I]: Paths E-M-J and E-P-J remain open.
    -   No pair of nodes can disconnect E and J.

Since a single node or a pair of nodes cannot disconnect E and J, and we found a set of 3 nodes that does, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

One possible cut set is [M, P, B]. Other valid cut sets of size 3 include [M, P, C] or [M, P, I], as C or I could also break the paths starting with E-B.

Step-by-step reasoning:

4.  **Consider a Cut Set of Size 3:** If we remove nodes L, B, and F, then E becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Since E is isolated, no path can exist between H and E. This forms a valid cut set: [L, B, F]. The size of this cut set is 3.
5.  **Attempt to Find a Smaller Cut Set (Size 2):** We need to determine if it's possible to disconnect H and E by removing only two nodes. Let's consider all possibilities for a 2-node cut set {N1, N2}.
    *   **Case 1: N1 and N2 are both direct neighbors of E (from {L, B, F}).**
        *   If we remove [L, B]: Paths from H to F (e.g., H -> G -> F -> E) still exist.
        *   If we remove [L, F]: Paths from H to B (e.g., H -> C -> B -> E) still exist.
        *   If we remove [B, F]: Paths from H to L (e.g., H -> I -> J -> M -> L -> E) still exist.
        *   Therefore, removing any two of {L, B, F} is not sufficient.
    *   **Case 2: N1 is a direct neighbor of E (from {L, B, F}), and N2 is another node.**
        *   Let's assume N1 = L is removed. Now we need N2 to disconnect H from both B and F.
            *   Paths to B: H -> C -> B -> E, H -> I -> J -> P -> O -> N -> B -> E, etc.
            *   Paths to F: H -> G -> F -> E, H -> X -> K -> F -> E, H -> X -> G -> F -> E.
            *   We need to find a single node N2 that breaks *all* remaining paths to B *and* *all* remaining paths to F.
            *   If N2 = C: Paths like H -> I -> J -> P -> O -> N -> B -> E (to B) and H -> G -> F -> E (to F) still exist.
            *   If N2 = I: Paths like H -> C -> B -> E (to B) and H -> G -> F -> E (to F) still exist.
            *   If N2 = G: Paths like H -> C -> B -> E (to B) and H -> X -> K -> F -> E (to F) still exist.
            *   If N2 = X: Paths like H -> C -> B -> E (to B) and H -> G -> F -> E (to F) still exist.
            *   No single node N2 can simultaneously cut all paths to B and all paths to F.
        *   The same logic applies if N1 = B or N1 = F.
    *   **Case 3: Neither N1 nor N2 are direct neighbors of E.**
        *   This means N1 and N2 must cut all paths leading to L, B, and F *before* they reach these nodes.
        *   Consider the shortest paths to each of E's neighbors:
            *   To L: H -> I -> J -> M -> L (requires cutting I, J, or M)
            *   To B: H -> C -> B (requires cutting C)
            *   To F: H -> G -> F (requires cutting G)
        *   To cut all three of these shortest paths, we would need to remove at least one node from {I, J, M}, one from {C}, and one from {G}. This clearly requires at least 3 nodes (e.g., [M, C, G]). Since we are limited to 2 nodes, it's impossible to cut all three of these independent "channels" with just two nodes. For example, if we remove I and C, the path H -> G -> F -> E still exists. If we remove I and G, the path H -> C -> B -> E still exists. If we remove C and G, the path H -> I -> J -> M -> L -> E still exists.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut set of size 2 is not possible, and a cut set of size 3 ([L, B, F]) is possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and J.
2.  **Identify all possible paths between E and J:** I will look for distinct paths, focusing on finding edge-disjoint paths to apply Menger's Theorem.
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H - I - J
        *   Edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (no common edges).
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common edges).

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** I have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and J is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Therefore, at least 3 edges must be removed.

4.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:** To achieve disconnection with 3 edges, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal breaks all identified paths. A common strategy is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that serve as "entry points" or "exit points" for paths.
    *   Consider the edges directly connected to J that are part of paths from E: (M, J), (I, J), and (P, J).
    *   Let's test this cut set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)].
        *   Removing (M, J) breaks Path 1 (E-M-J) and any other path going through M to J (e.g., E-F-X-K-M-J, E-B-H-G-X-K-M-J).
        *   Removing (I, J) breaks Path 2 (E-F-G-H-I-J) and any other path going through I to J (e.g., E-B-H-I-J, E-B-C-I-J).
        *   Removing (P, J) breaks Path 3 (E-B-C-O-P-J) and any other path going through P to J (e.g., E-B-N-C-O-P-J).

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   Each edge in the cut set [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)] is necessary because each of the three edge-disjoint paths identified in step 2 relies on one of these edges to reach J. If any of these edges were left, a path would still exist.
    *   Since we found a cut set of 3 edges, and we determined that at least 3 edges are required (from Menger's Theorem), 3 is indeed the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 9
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, B and M.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are B and M.

2.  **Identify direct connections to M:** Node M is directly connected to three other nodes: E, F, and L. This means any path from B to M must pass through one of these three edges: (E, M), (F, M), or (L, M).

3.  **Consider a simple cut set:** A straightforward approach to disconnect M from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to M. This would be the set `[(E, M), (F, M), (L, M)]`.
    *   Removing (E, M) breaks any path ending with E-M.
    *   Removing (F, M) breaks any path ending with F-M.
    *   Removing (L, M) breaks any path ending with L-M.
    Since E, F, and L are the only neighbors of M, removing these three edges guarantees that M is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from B. This cut set has a size of 3.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set is possible (e.g., size 1 or 2):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we can try to find edge-disjoint paths between B and M. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least *k*.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to M:
    *   **Path 1:** B - E - M
        Edges: `(B, E)`, `(E, M)`
    *   **Path 2:** B - N - L - M
        Edges: `(B, N)`, `(N, L)`, `(L, M)`
    *   **Path 3:** B - H - G - F - M
        Edges: `(B, H)`, `(H, G)`, `(G, F)`, `(F, M)`

    Let's check if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses edges `(B, E)` and `(E, M)`.
    *   Path 2 uses edges `(B, N)`, `(N, L)`, and `(L, M)`.
    *   Path 3 uses edges `(B, H)`, `(H, G)`, `(G, F)`, and `(F, M)`.

    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between these three paths. They are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M, Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect B and M is at least 3.

6.  **Conclusion:** We have found a cut set of size 3 (`[(E, M), (F, M), (L, M)]`) that successfully disconnects B and M, and we have proven that at least 3 edges must be removed. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (F, M), (L, M)]
